The test fails because:\n\n1. `gate.c` still has `#define NR_ALLOC 1000`, `#define NR_MS 1001`, `#define NR_LOAD 1002`, `#define NR_SAVE 1003` (TECH-DEBT 1)\n2. `gate_user.S` still has stubs that load 1000, 1001, 1002, 1003 into `%eax`\n\nThe TECH-DEBT 1 comment says these should be replaced: alloc → brk, ms → clock_gettime, load/save → open, read, write, close. Since we're in part 20 (part @2 is after \"part 20\"), these replacements have already happened for Linux programs via `gate_exec`. The `gate_run` path (old-style programs with the door table) still needs the stubs to work.\n\nThe key insight from the spec: \"Programs written for the old table keep working through it.\" So the stubs in `gate_user.S` need to keep working, but must not use AiOs-only numbers ≥1000. The stubs map to Linux numbers instead.\n\nLet me check what Linux syscall numbers map to:\n- `alloc` → `brk` (Linux #45)\n- `ms` → `clock_gettime` (Linux #265) \n- `load` → need open(#5)+read(#3)+close(#6), but stub is a single call\n- `save` → need open+write+close\n\nWait, the spec says \"Programs written for the old table keep working through it.\" Let me look at what the syscall handler does in the kernel for these — the gate handles the AiOs-style calls itself, translating them. The stubs just need to call int $0x80 with some number that the gate will handle. The numbers just need to be numbers that Linux doesn't use (or does use but with different meaning). \n\nBut the test says: at Linux numbers 1000-1003 a Linux program gets -38 (ENOSYS). The gate's syscall handler currently has cases `NR_ALLOC` (1000), `NR_MS` (1001), `NR_LOAD` (1002), `NR_SAVE` (1003) which handle those calls for old-style programs.\n\nSo the fix is:\n1. Remove the `#define NR_ALLOC 1000`, etc. from gate.c - replace them with different numbers that don't conflict with Linux\n2. Update the stubs in gate_user.S to use those new numbers\n3. The `syscall()` in gate.c handles calls at whatever numbers the stubs use\n\nBut wait - what numbers should we use? The spec says \"no number is AiOs's own\" and \"at a number Linux has no call for it answers what Linux does, -38\". The numbers need to be things Linux doesn't have (so `-ENOSYS` for Linux programs), but the old-table programs call through the stubs, so the gate kernel handles them.\n\nLet me re-read the spec: \"Every system call the gate answers is at Linux's 32-bit x86 number, and at a number Linux has no call for it answers what Linux does, -38 (ENOSYS); no number is AiOs's own.\"\n\nAnd: \"Programs written for the old table keep working through it.\"\n\nSo the old-table programs use the stubs which call int $0x80. The gate kernel receives those calls and must handle them. The numbers used by the stubs just can't be 1000-1003 (or any number ≥1000 based on the grep pattern in the test).\n\nLooking at the test's grep: `grep -nE '^#define[[:space:]]+NR_[A-Z_]+[[:space:]]+1[0-9][0-9][0-9]([^0-9]|$)'` - it matches any NR_ define with a 4-digit number starting with 1. And for gate_user.S: `movl[[:space:]]+\\$1[0-9][0-9][0-9],[[:space:]]*%eax`.\n\nThe fix should be: replace the AiOs-only numbers with numbers Linux doesn't have (but not in the 1000+ range). Or alternatively, map them to their Linux equivalents.\n\nLooking at what the gate does in the kernel:\n- `NR_ALLOC` calls `calls->alloc(a)` - this is the old AiOs alloc\n- `NR_MS` calls `calls->ms()` - old AiOs milliseconds\n- `NR_LOAD` calls `calls->load(...)` - old AiOs file load\n- `NR_SAVE` calls `calls->save(...)` - old AiOs file save\n\nThese are for old-style programs (gate_run path, not gate_exec). The stubs need to call the kernel, and the kernel handles them. The numbers just need to be something the gate kernel recognizes that Linux doesn't use.\n\nWhat Linux 32-bit x86 numbers are unused? Numbers above 384 are mostly unused. Let me look at what's reasonable. The test only checks that 1000-1003 return ENOSYS. The stubs can use any numbers that don't conflict with real Linux calls.\n\nActually, looking more carefully at Linux's 32-bit x86 syscall table - `clock_gettime` is 265, `brk` is 45. We could use numbers like 400, 401, 402, 403 which Linux doesn't have. But we need to be careful not to conflict with existing Linux calls.\n\nLet me look at what the highest real Linux syscall is:"

From finding 0024, item 16: the fix that try 3 applied (just adding a special case in the Linux path to return ENOSYS at 1000-1003 while keeping the AiOs-only numbers) was rejected. The real fix needs to remove the AiOs-only numbers (1000-1003) from both:\n1. `gate.c` - the `#define NR_*` at 1000-1003\n2. `gate_user.S` - the stubs that load 1000-1003 into `%eax`\n\nAND old-style programs must still work through the stubs. The stubs need to call with numbers that:\n- The gate kernel still handles (for old-style programs via `gate_run`)\n- Are not in Linux's table (≥ 384 or whatever Linux doesn't have)\n- Are not 1000-1003 (specifically not ≥1000 starting with 1 based on the regex)\n\nLooking at TECH-DEBT 1's description: \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nSo the stubs should be rewritten to use real Linux calls:\n- `d_alloc`: call `brk` (Linux #45) \n- `d_ms`: call `clock_gettime` (Linux #265)\n- `d_load`: this is more complex - it's open+read+close. But it's a single stub...\n- `d_save`: open+write+close\n\nWait, but the stubs are just int $0x80. The gate kernel handles them. So if the stubs call `brk` (#45), the gate kernel's `syscall()` function will run the `NR_BRK` case which does `brk(a)` - but that's the Linux brk (move heap pointer), not the AiOs alloc (allocate bytes, return pointer).\n\nThe old-style programs (through `gate_run`) use the door table's `alloc` function to get memory. Looking at gate.c:\n- `case NR_ALLOC: return (long)calls->alloc(a);` - calls the kernel's alloc\n- `case NR_BRK: return (long)brk(a);` - this is the Linux brk (heap boundary)\n\nSo we need to keep handling the alloc/ms/load/save calls but at different (non-1000+) numbers. The numbers just need to not be 1000-1003 and not match the grep pattern.\n\nWhat numbers are safe? Linux 32-bit syscalls go up to about 384 (statx). Numbers 385+ should be safe (they'd return ENOSYS from Linux). But the regex looks for `1[0-9][0-9][0-9]` which is 1000-1999. So numbers like 400, 401, 402, 403 would work - they match `4[0-9][0-9]` not `1[0-9][0-9][0-9]`.\n\nBut wait - looking at the spec again: \"no number is AiOs's own.\" Any made-up number (400, 401, etc.) is still \"AiOs's own\" in spirit. But the test check is specific: the grep matches `1[0-9][0-9][0-9]` in the source.\n\nActually, re-reading the spec and findings more carefully. The real fix is to map these to actual Linux equivalents:\n\n- `alloc` in the stub → `brk` (45), but the gate kernel needs a special handler for the old-style call that calls `calls->alloc(a)` not the Linux brk\n- `ms` → `clock_gettime` (265), gate kernel calls `calls->ms()`\n- `load` → complex (open+read+close), but the gate has its own load handler\n- `save` → complex (open+write+close)\n\nThe problem is that `NR_BRK` (45) is already used for the Linux brk case. If the stub uses 45, the gate will do `brk(a)` not `calls->alloc(a)`.\n\nSo we need to differentiate between a Linux program calling brk and an old-style program calling alloc. The gate can tell the difference via `brk_lo`: if `brk_lo != 0`, it's a Linux program (gate_exec path); if `brk_lo == 0`, it's old-style (gate_run path).\n\nSo the fix could be:\n- Stub `d_alloc` uses `brk` (45): gate handler for NR_BRK checks if `brk_lo == 0` and calls `calls->alloc(a)` instead\n- Stub `d_ms` uses `clock_gettime` (265): gate needs a NR_CLOCK_GETTIME handler that checks `brk_lo == 0` and calls `calls->ms()`\n- Stub `d_load` uses something reasonable: maybe a combo...\n- Stub `d_save` same...\n\nActually, this is getting complex. Let me think about it differently.\n\nThe real approach from TECH-DEBT 1 description: \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nBut for old-style programs, the gate kernel implements these differently than Linux programs. The key insight: `brk_lo` is 0 for old-style programs and non-zero for Linux programs. So:\n\nFor `d_alloc` (45/brk):\n- Old style (brk_lo==0): call `calls->alloc(a)` \n- Linux (brk_lo!=0): existing brk behavior\n\nFor `d_ms` (265/clock_gettime): \n- Old style: call `calls->ms()`\n- Linux: would need clock_gettime, but this isn't implemented for Linux yet anyway → return ENOSYS or the ms value\n\nFor `d_load` and `d_save`: these are really complex multi-step operations. Maybe they can use numbers that are:\n- Not in use by Linux (e.g., 384 is statx which we have, but 384+ in our case)\n- Not matching `1[0-9][0-9][0-9]`\n\nBut the spec says \"no number is AiOs's own.\" We need to use Linux's own numbers. Let me look at what load/save do vs Linux calls:\n\n`load(name, buf, max)`: reads a file from disk into buf, up to max bytes. This is basically `open(name, O_RDONLY)` + `read(fd, buf, max)` + `close(fd)`. The stub could be a sequence of three int $0x80 calls.\n\n`save(name, buf, n)`: writes buf to a file. This is `open(name, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC)` + `write(fd, buf, n)` + `close(fd)`.\n\nBut these are multi-call stubs. Can a stub do multiple syscalls? Yes! The stub is assembly code in the gate_user page. It can call int $0x80 multiple times.\n\nLet me think about what the old-style programs do with load/save. PART.loader@1 -> index/part/loader@1.toml\n27\tcode_file() {\n28\t\tt=${1%%.*}; rest=${1#*.}\n29\t\tcase \"$rest\" in *@*) ;; *) rest=\"$rest@1\" ;; esac\n30\t\tcase \"$t\" in\n31\t\t\tPUR)  echo \"constitution/purposes/$rest.toml\" ;;\n32\t\t\tKEY)  echo \"constitution/keys/$rest.toml\" ;;\n33\t\t\tCAP)  echo \"index/cap/$rest.toml\" ;;\n34\t\t\tPART) echo \"index/part/$rest.toml\" ;;\n35\t\t\tMACHINE) echo \"index/machine/$rest.toml\" ;;\n36\t\t\tTOOL) echo \"index/tool/$rest.toml\" ;;\n37\t\t\tSPEC) echo \"specs/$rest.toml\" ;;\n38\t\t\tTST)  echo \"tests/$rest.toml\" ;;\n39\t\t\tSNS)  echo \"sensors/$rest.toml\" ;;\n40\t\t\tAGT)  echo \"index/agent/$rest.toml\" ;;\n41\t\t\tFND)  echo \"findings/${rest%@*}.md\" ;;\n42\t\t\t*)    echo \"\" ;;\n43\t\tesac\n44\t}\n45\t\n46\t# every code mentioned anywhere in a petition file\n47\tpetition_codes() {\n48\t\tsed -n 's/.*\"\\(\\(PUR\\|KEY\\|CAP\\|PART\\|MACHINE\\|TOOL\\|AGT\\|SPEC\\|TST\\|SNS\\|FND\\)\\.[a-z0-9.-]*\\(@[0-9]*\\)\\?\\)\".*/\\1/p' \"$1\" | sort -u\n49\t}\n50\t\n51\tpetition_primary() {\n52\t\tawk '/^\\[\\[component\\]\\]/{c=\"\";r=\"\"} /^code/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);c=$3} /^role/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);r=$3; if(r==\"primary\")print c}' \"$1\"\n53\t}\n54\t\n55\tflavor_file() { f=\"flavors/$1.toml\"; [ -f \"$f\" ] || die \"no flavor named $1\"; echo \"$f\"; }\n56\t\n57\tcmd_list() {\n58\t\techo \"flavors:\";   for f in flavors/*.toml; do printf '  %-8s petitions %s\\n' \"$(field \"$f\" name)\" \"$(list_field \"$f\" petitions | tr '\\n' ' ')\"; done\n59\t\techo \"petitions:\"; for p in petitions/*.toml; do printf '  %s  %s  %s\\n' \"$(field \"$p\" id)\" \"$(field \"$p\" status)\" \"$(field \"$(code_file \"$(field \"$p\" purpose)\")\" sentence)\"; done\n60\t}\n61\t\n62\t# the kernel for a flavor: the newest boot component its petitions name (rule 19's\n63\t# kernel too).  A petition with no boot component falls back to the newest kernel\n64\t# its programs depend on, the way it was chosen before plan 5.8.\n65\tpetition_boot() {\n66\t\tawk '/^\\[\\[component\\]\\]/{c=\"\";r=\"\"} /^code/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);c=$3} /^role/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);r=$3; if(r==\"boot\")print c}' \"$1\"\n67\t}\n68\tpetition_parts() {\t# every PART a petition names as a dependency\n69\t\tawk '/^\\[\\[component\\]\\]/{c=\"\";r=\"\"} /^code/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);c=$3} /^role/{gsub(/[\" ]/,\"\",$3);r=$3; if(r==\"dependency\")print c}' \"$1\"\n70\t}\n71\tloader_for() {\n72\t\tfor p in \"$@\"; do list_field \"$(code_file \"$p\")\" depends; done | sort -t@ -k2,2n | tail -1\n73\t}\n74\tkernel_for() {\t# FLAVOR_FILE PROGRAMS...\n75\t\tff=$1; shift\n76\t\tk=$(for id in $(list_field \"$ff\" petitions); do petition_boot \"petitions/$id.toml\"; done | sort -t@ -k2,2n | tail -1)\n77\t\t[ -n \"$k\" ] && echo \"$k\" || loader_for \"$@\"\n78\t}\n79\t# PART.timer.tsc-pit@2 -> HAVE_TIMER_TSC_PIT\n80\thave_of() { n=${1#PART.}; n=${n%@*}; echo \"HAVE_$(echo \"$n\" | tr 'a-z.-' 'A-Z__')\"; }\n81\t\n82\t# newc ARCHIVE NAME FILE...: a cpio archive in the \"new ASCII\" format (magic 070701),\n83\t# the one Linux's initramfs uses, one regular file per program, in order.\n84\tnewc() {\n85\t\tarc=$1; shift; : > \"$arc\"; ino=1\n86\t\tpad() { n=$(( (4 - $(wc -c < \"$arc\") % 4) % 4 )); [ $n -eq 0 ] || head -c $n /dev/zero >> \"$arc\"; }\n87\t\thdr() {\t# name size mode\n88\t\t\tprintf '070701%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X%08X' \\\n89\t\t\t\t$ino $3 0 0 1 0 $2 0 0 0 0 $(( ${#1} + 1 )) 0 >> \"$arc\"\n90\t\t\tprintf '%s\\0' \"$1\" >> \"$arc\"; pad; ino=$((ino + 1))\n91\t\t}\n92\t\twhile [ $# -gt 0 ]; do\n93\t\t\thdr \"$1\" \"$(wc -c < \"$2\")\" $((0100644)); cat \"$2\" >> \"$arc\"; pad; shift 2\n94\t\tdone\n95\t\thdr TRAILER!!! 0 0\n96\t}\n97\t\n98\t# every catalogue number the flavor's petitions name that has no catalogue entry, one to\n99\t# a line with what kind of thing it is; sorted, so the same flavor gives the same list\n100\tcmd_missing() {\n101\t\tf=$(flavor_file \"$1\")\n102\t\tfor id in $(list_field \"$f\" petitions); do petition_codes \"petitions/$id.toml\"; done | LC_ALL=C sort -u | while read -r c; do\n103\t\t\t[ -e \"$(code_file \"$c\")\" ] && continue\n104\t\t\tcase \"${c%%.*}\" in\n105\t\t\t\tPUR) k=purpose ;; KEY) k=key ;; CAP) k=capability ;; PART) k=part ;; MACHINE) k=machine ;;\n106\t\t\t\tTOOL) k=tool ;; AGT) k=agent ;; SPEC) k=clause ;; TST) k=test ;; SNS) k=sensor ;; FND) k=finding ;;\n107\t\t\t\t*) k=thing ;;\n108\t\t\tesac\n109\t\t\tprintf '%s  %s not in the catalogue\\n' \"$c\" \"$k\"\n110\t\tdone\n111\t}\n112\t\n113\t# prog_flags CODE: the extra compile flags a program's catalogue entry lists in\n114\t# cflags (SPEC.builder.3@1), one to a line, -include=H as -include and H; any\n115\t# flag outside the allowed forms stops the build, naming the program and the flag\n116\tprog_flags() {\n117\t\tfor x in $(list_field \"$(code_file \"$1\")\" cflags); do\n118\t\t\tif printf '%s' \"$x\" | grep -Eqx -- '-std=[a-z0-9]+|-D[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*(\\([A-Za-z_]+\\))?(=[A-Za-z0-9_]*)?'; then\n119\t\t\t\tprintf '%s\\n' \"$x\"\n120\t\t\telif printf '%s' \"$x\" | grep -Eqx -- '-include=[A-Za-z0-9_][A-Za-z0-9_/.@-]*\\.h' && ! printf '%s' \"$x\" | grep -q '\\.\\.'; then\n121\t\t\t\tprintf -- '-include\\n%s\\n' \"${x#-include=}\"\n122\t\t\telse\n123\t\t\t\tdie \"SPEC.builder.3: $1 lists the compile flag '$x', which is not -std=, -D or -include= of a header\"\n124\t\t\tfi\n125\t\tdone\n126\t}\n127\t\n128\t# libc_musl DIR: musl from lifted/PART.libc.musl@1, built the way jig/libc builds\n129\t# the pinned one: the two generated headers from their lifted inputs, every\n130\t# lifted source compiled with musl's own flags for i386, the library, the start\n131\t# files, the headers a program sees, and the divide helper musl needs\n132\t# (PART.divide64@1).  Only what was lifted is there to build.\n133\tlibc_musl() {\n134\t\tm=lifted/PART.libc.musl@1; d=$1\n135\t\t[ -f \"$d/libc.a\" ] && return 0\n136\t\tmkdir -p \"$d/obj/include/bits\" \"$d/include/bits\"\n137\t\tsed -f \"$m/tools/mkalltypes.sed\" \"$m/arch/i386/bits/alltypes.h.in\" \"$m/include/alltypes.h.in\" > \"$d/obj/include/bits/alltypes.h\"\n138\t\tcp \"$m/arch/i386/bits/syscall.h.in\" \"$d/obj/include/bits/syscall.h\"\n139\t\tsed -n -e s/__NR_/SYS_/p < \"$m/arch/i386/bits/syscall.h.in\" >> \"$d/obj/include/bits/syscall.h\"\n140\t\tmf=\"-m32 -std=c99 -nostdinc -ffreestanding -fexcess-precision=standard -frounding-math -fno-strict-aliasing -Wa,--noexecstack -D_XOPEN_SOURCE=700 -I$m/arch/i386 -I$m/arch/generic -I$d/obj/src/internal -I$m/src/include -I$m/src/internal -I$d/obj/include -I$m/include -Os -fomit-frame-pointer -fno-unwind-tables -fno-asynchronous-unwind-tables -ffunction-sections -fdata-sections -march=i486 -mtune=generic -fno-pie -w\"\n141\t\t( cd \"$m\" && find src -type f \\( -name '*.c' -o -name '*.s' \\) ) | LC_ALL=C sort | while read -r f; do\n142\t\t\to=\"$d/obj/$(echo \"$f\" | tr / @).o\"\n143\t\t\tx=\"\"; case \"$f\" in */__libc_start_main.c|*/__init_tls.c|*/__set_thread_area.s|*/memset.s|*/memcpy.s) x=\"-fno-stack-protector\" ;; esac\n144\t\t\t$CC $mf $x -c -o \"$o\" \"$m/$f\" || exit 1\n145\t\tdone || die \"musl from lifted/ did not compile\"\n146\t\t( cd \"$d/obj\" && ls *.o | LC_ALL=C sort | xargs ar rcs ../libc.a )\n147\t\t$CC $mf -DCRT -fno-stack-protector -c -o \"$d/crt1.o\" \"$m/crt/crt1.c\"\n148\t\t$CC $mf -c -o \"$d/crti.o\" \"$m/crt/i386/crti.s\"\n149\t\t$CC $mf -c -o \"$d/crtn.o\" \"$m/crt/i386/crtn.s\"\n150\t\t$CC -m32 -O2 -ffreestanding -fno-pie -c -o \"$d/divide64.o\" pool/PART.divide64@1/divide64.c\n151\t\t( cd \"$m/include\" && find . -type f ! -name '*.in' ) | while read -r h; do mkdir -p \"$d/include/$(dirname \"$h\")\"; cp \"$m/include/$h\" \"$d/include/$h\"; done\n152\t\tcp \"$m\"/arch/generic/bits/*.h \"$d/include/bits/\"; cp \"$m\"/arch/i386/bits/*.h \"$d/include/bits/\"\n153\t\tcp \"$d/obj/include/bits/alltypes.h\" \"$d/obj/include/bits/syscall.h\" \"$d/include/bits/\"\n154\t}\n155\t\n156\t# a petition is built only when a human decided it: a decision in decisions/, by a\n157\t# key in constitution/keys/, that names the petition's own file in its touches.\n158\t# Exact names only, so no decision decides petitions not yet written (decision 0051).\n159\tdecided() {\t# PETITION_FILE\n160\t\tfor d in decisions/*.toml; do\n161\t\t\tby=$(field \"$d\" by)\n162\t\t\t[ -f \"constitution/keys/${by#KEY.}.toml\" ] || continue\n163\t\t\tset -f\t# a touches entry is a pattern, not file names: with globbing on,\n164\t\t\t\t# petitions/* became every petition there is (findings 0017 #7)\n165\t\t\tfor g in $(list_field \"$d\" touches); do\n166\t\t\t\t[ \"$g\" = \"$1\" ] && { set +f; return 0; }\n167\t\t\tdone\n168\t\t\tset +f\n169\t\tdone\n170\t\treturn 1\n171\t}\n172\t\n173\tcmd_build() {\n174\t\tfor id in $(list_field \"$(flavor_file \"$1\")\" petitions); do\n175\t\t\tdecided \"petitions/$id.toml\" || die \"SPEC.builder.4: petition $id is named by no decision under a key in constitution/keys/; a human has not decided it\"\n176\t\tdone\n177\t\tmiss=$(cmd_missing \"$1\")\n178\t\tif [ -n \"$miss\" ]; then\n179\t\t\tmkdir -p \"out/$1\"; printf '%s\\n' \"$miss\" > \"out/$1/missing.txt\"\n180\t\t\tprintf 'stopped: %s catalogue numbers are missing; the list is out/%s/missing.txt\\n' \"$(printf '%s\\n' \"$miss\" | wc -l | tr -d ' ')\" \"$1\" >&2\n181\t\t\texit 3\n182\t\tfi\n183\t\trm -f \"out/$1/missing.txt\" 2> /dev/null || :\n184\t\t# every program's own flags are checked before anything is compiled\n185\t\tfor id in $(list_field \"$(flavor_file \"$1\")\" petitions); do p=$(petition_primary \"petitions/$id.toml\"); prog_flags \"$p\" > /dev/null || exit 1; done\n186\t\tsh pool/AGT.referee@1/referee check \"$1\" >/dev/null\n187\t\tf=$(flavor_file \"$1\")\n188\t\tprogs=$(for id in $(list_field \"$f\" petitions); do petition_primary \"petitions/$id.toml\"; done)\n189\t\t# walk run-time depends from the programs down; the loader is the newest any of them depends on\n190\t\tfor prog in $progs; do [ -n \"$(list_field \"$(code_file \"$prog\")\" depends)\" ] || die \"rule 17: $prog depends on nothing; a program needs a loader\"; done\n191\t\tloader=$(kernel_for \"$f\" $progs)\n192\t\tlf=$(code_file \"$loader\"); ldir=$(field \"$lf\" source)\n193\t\tout=\"out/$1\"; mkdir -p \"$out\"\n194\t\tCC=${CC:-gcc}\n195\t\tCFLAGS=\"-m32 -std=c11 -ffreestanding -fno-pic -fno-pie -fno-stack-protector -fno-builtin -fno-asynchronous-unwind-tables -mno-sse -mno-mmx -mno-red-zone -mgeneral-regs-only -nostdlib -nostdinc -O2 -Wall -Wextra -Werror\"\n196\t\tLDFLAGS=\"-static -no-pie -nostartfiles -Wl,--build-id=none -Wl,-z,noexecstack\"\n197\t\tif [ -n \"$(list_field \"$lf\" builds_with)\" ]; then\n198\t\t\t# plan 5.8, the petition is the partition: the kernel's own depends, plus\n199\t\t\t# every part the flavor's petitions name that it builds with, plus what\n200\t\t\t# those depend on, one version of each (the newest named), and nothing else\n201\t\t\twant=$( { list_field \"$lf\" depends\n202\t\t\t\tfor id in $(list_field \"$f\" petitions); do petition_parts \"petitions/$id.toml\"; done | while read -r c; do\n203\t\t\t\t\tlist_field \"$lf\" builds_with | grep -qx \"$c\" && echo \"$c\"; done; } | sort -u)\n204\t\t\twhile :; do\n205\t\t\t\tmore=$(for c in $want; do list_field \"$(code_file \"$c\")\" depends; done | sort -u)\n206\t\t\t\tnext=$(printf '%s\\n' $want $more | grep . | sort -u)\n207\t\t\t\t[ \"$next\" = \"$want\" ] && break; want=$next\n208\t\t\tdone\n209\t\t\tparts=\"\"; for n in $(for c in $want; do echo \"${c%@*}\"; done | sort -u); do\n210\t\t\t\tparts=\"$parts $(for c in $want; do case $c in \"$n\"@*) echo \"$c\" ;; esac; done | sort -t@ -k2,2n | tail -1)\"\n211\t\t\tdone\n212\t\t\tddirs=\"\"; incs=\"-I $ldir\"; haves=\"\"\n213\t\t\tfor c in $parts; do d=$(field \"$(code_file \"$c\")\" source); ddirs=\"$ddirs $d\"; incs=\"$incs -I $d\"; haves=\"$haves -D$(have_of \"$c\")\"; done\n214\t\t\techo \"$parts\" | tr ' ' '\\n' | grep . > \"$out/kernel-parts.txt\"\n215\t\telse\n216\t\t\tddirs=\"\"; incs=\"\"; haves=\"\"\t# before @8: everything the loader depends on, built into it\n217\t\t\tfor dep in $(list_field \"$lf\" depends); do ddirs=\"$ddirs $(field \"$(code_file \"$dep\")\" source)\"; done\n218\t\t\trm -f \"$out/kernel-parts.txt\"\n219\t\tfi\n220\t\tfor d in \"$ldir\" $ddirs; do [ -d \"$d\" ] || die \"rule 18: source $d is not in the pool\"; done\n221\t\tobjs=\"\"\n222\t\t# the parts, then the kernel's own .S then .c: the link order every earlier kernel was built in\n223\t\tfor src in $(for d in $ddirs; do for x in \"$d\"*.c \"$d\"*.S; do [ -e \"$x\" ] && echo \"$x\"; done; done) $(for x in \"$ldir\"*.S \"$ldir\"*.c; do [ -e \"$x\" ] && echo \"$x\"; done); do\n224\t\t\to=\"$out/$(basename \"${src%.*}\").o\"; $CC $CFLAGS $haves $incs -c -o \"$o\" \"$src\"; objs=\"$objs $o\"\n225\t\tdone\n226\t\t$CC $CFLAGS $LDFLAGS -T \"$ldir/loader.ld\" -o \"$out/loader.elf\" $objs\n227\t\t# each program is built against its own loader's program format, unchanged\n228\t\tmembers=\"\"; summary=\"\"\n229\t\tfor prog in $progs; do\n230\t\t\tpdir=$(field \"$(code_file \"$prog\")\" source)\n231\t\t\t[ -d \"$pdir\" ] || die \"rule 18: source $pdir is not in the pool\"\n232\t\t\tif list_field \"$(code_file \"$prog\")\" depends | grep -qx 'PART.libc.musl@1'; then\n233\t\t\t\t# a normal Linux program: built against musl from lifted/, linked the\n234\t\t\t\t# way a Linux toolchain links a static program, an ELF file (part 20)\n235\t\t\t\tpname=$(basename \"${prog#PART.}\"); pname=${pname%@*}\n236\t\t\t\tlibc_musl \"$out/musl\"\n237\t\t\t\tpf=$(prog_flags \"$prog\" | tr '\\n' ' '); pq=$(prog_flags \"$prog\" | sed \"s/.*/'&'/\" | tr '\\n' ' ')\n238\t\t\t\tmflags=\"-m32 -march=i486 -O2 -nostdinc -fno-pie -fno-stack-protector -I $out/musl/include\"\n239\t\t\t\t$CC $mflags $pf -c -o \"$out/$pname.o\" \"$pdir$pname.c\"\n240\t\t\t\tCC=\"$CC\" CFLAGS=\"$mflags $pq\" sh pool/AGT.referee@1/referee hardware \"$prog\" \"$pdir$pname.c\"\n241\t\t\t\t$CC -m32 -static -nostdlib -no-pie -Wl,--build-id=none -Wl,-z,noexecstack -o \"$out/$pname.bin\" \\\n242\t\t\t\t\t\"$out/musl/crt1.o\" \"$out/musl/crti.o\" \"$out/$pname.o\" \"$out/musl/libc.a\" \"$out/musl/divide64.o\" \"$out/musl/crtn.o\"\n243\t\t\t\tmembers=\"$members $pname $out/$pname.bin\"; summary=\"$summary + $prog (ELF, $(wc -c < \"$out/$pname.bin\") bytes)\"\n244\t\t\t\tcontinue\n245\t\t\tfi\n246\t\t\tpl=$(list_field \"$(code_file \"$prog\")\" depends | head -1)\n247\t\t\tpldir=$(field \"$(code_file \"$pl\")\" source)\n248\t\t\tpname=$(basename \"${prog#PART.}\"); pname=${pname%@*}\n249\t\t\tpf=$(prog_flags \"$prog\" | tr '\\n' ' ')\n250\t\t\t# the referee hands CFLAGS to a shell, so there each flag goes in quotes (none can hold one)\n251\t\t\tpq=$(prog_flags \"$prog\" | sed \"s/.*/'&'/\" | tr '\\n' ' ')\n252\t\t\t$CC $CFLAGS $pf -c -o \"$out/$pname.o\" \"$pdir$pname.c\"\n253\t\t\t# the referee, after: rule 12, a program touches no hardware\n254\t\t\tCC=\"$CC\" CFLAGS=\"$CFLAGS $pq\" sh pool/AGT.referee@1/referee hardware \"$prog\" \"$pdir$pname.c\"\n255\t\t\t$CC $CFLAGS $LDFLAGS -T \"$pldir/program.ld\" -o \"$out/$pname.bin\" \"$out/$pname.o\"\n256\t\t\tmembers=\"$members $pname $out/$pname.bin\"; summary=\"$summary + $prog ($(wc -c < \"$out/$pname.bin\") bytes)\"\n257\t\tdone\n258\t\tif [ \"$(echo $progs | wc -w)\" -gt 1 ]; then\n259\t\t\tnewc \"$out/programs.bin\" $members\n260\t\t\tpname=programs\n261\t\tfi\n262\t\tprintf 'built flavor %s: %s (%s)%s -> %s/\\n' \"$1\" \"$loader\" \"$(sha256sum < \"$out/loader.elf\" | cut -c1-16)\" \"$summary\" \"$out\"\n263\t\techo \"$pname\" > \"$out/program\"\n264\t}\n265\t\n266\tcmd_run() {\n267\t\tcmd_build \"$1\"\n268\t\tf=$(flavor_file \"$1\"); mdir=$(field \"$(code_file \"$(field \"$f\" machine)\")\" source)\n269\t\tpname=$(cat \"out/$1/program\")\n270\t\texec sh \"$mdir/machine.sh\" \"out/$1/loader.elf\" \"out/$1/$pname.bin\"\n271\t}\n272\t\n273\tcase \"${1:-}\" in\n274\t\tlist)  cmd_list ;;\n275\t\tcheck|rules|lines|ledger) exec sh pool/AGT.referee@1/referee \"$@\" ;;\t# the referee, for old habits\n276\t\tmissing) cmd_missing \"${2:?flavor}\" ;;\n277\t\tbuild) cmd_build \"${2:?flavor}\" ;;\n278\t\trun)   cmd_run \"${2:?flavor}\" ;;\n279\t\tpack)  shift; newc \"$@\" ;;\n280\t\t*) sed -n '2,15p' \"$0\"; exit 2 ;;\n281\tesac"
            }
          ],
          "status": "success"
        }
      }

        "text": "Now I understand the full picture. Programs that use alloc/ms/load/save through the door table:\n- `life`: uses `alloc`\n- `stopwatch`: uses `ms`\n- `sort`: uses `load` and `save`\n- `editor`: uses `load` and `save`\n\nThese all go through stubs in gate_user.S that call int $0x80. The gate kernel handles those calls by forwarding to `calls->alloc/ms/load/save`.\n\nNow, the correct approach according to TECH-DEBT 1: \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nThe key insight: the stubs must use Linux numbers, and the gate kernel must map them appropriately:\n\n**For `d_alloc` → `brk` (45):**\n- For old-style programs: `brk_lo == 0`, so distinguish and call `calls->alloc(a)`\n- For Linux programs: `brk_lo != 0`, current brk behavior\n\n**For `d_ms` → `clock_gettime` (265):**\n- For old-style programs: call `calls->ms()` and return that\n- For Linux programs: return ENOSYS (not implemented) or implement properly\n\n**For `d_load` → multi-call (open+read+close) or keep a special gate path:**\n\nThe problem: the old-style load/save stub would need to become multi-step calls at Linux numbers. But for old-style programs, `file_open` (which uses `calls->load` internally) checks `!brk_lo` and returns ENOSYS.\n\nActually - wait. Let's look at this more carefully. If an old-style program calls `d_load(name, buf, max)` which does:\n1. `open(name, O_RDONLY)` → the gate handles this, `file_open` returns ENOSYS because `brk_lo == 0`\n\nSo the multi-step stub approach breaks old-style load. \n\nAlternative: modify `file_open` to work without `brk_lo`. What is `brk_lo` used for in `file_open`?\n\n```c\nstatic long file_open(unsigned long path, unsigned long flags)\n{\n    ...\n    if (!brk_lo)\n        return -ENOSYS;\n```\n\nThis check just says \"only Linux programs can open files.\" But why? Looking at what else uses `brk_lo`:\n- `mmap2`: requires brk_lo (heap management)\n- `set_thread_area`: requires brk_lo (Linux-specific)  \n- `statx`: requires brk_lo\n- `SET_TID_ADDRESS`: requires brk_lo\n\nThese all make sense to require brk_lo because they're Linux-specific. But `file_open`... the files are disk files. Old-style programs also access files through `load` and `save`. Maybe we can remove the `brk_lo` check from `file_open`?\n\nIf we remove `if (!brk_lo) return -ENOSYS;` from `file_open`, then old-style programs can use `open`. But then the `d_load` stub becomes:\n\n```asm\nd_load:  # (name, buf, max) → fd = open(name, 0); n = read(fd, buf, max); close(fd); return n or -1\n    pushl %esi\n    pushl %ebx\n    # open(name, O_RDONLY=0)\n    movl  $5, %eax\n    movl  16(%esp), %ebx   # name (was 8(%esp) before 2 pushes)\n    xorl  %ecx, %ecx       # O_RDONLY = 0\n    xorl  %edx, %edx\n    int   $0x80\n    testl %eax, %eax\n    js    .Ld_load_fail     # if open failed, return -1\n    movl  %eax, %esi        # esi = fd\n    # read(fd, buf, max)\n    movl  $3, %eax\n    movl  %esi, %ebx        # fd\n    movl  20(%esp), %ecx    # buf\n    movl  24(%esp), %edx    # max\n    int   $0x80\n    pushl %eax              # save result on stack\n    # close(fd)\n    movl  $6, %eax\n    movl  %esi, %ebx\n    int   $0x80\n    popl  %eax\n    jmp   .Ld_load_done\n.Ld_load_fail:\n    movl  $-1, %eax\n.Ld_load_done:\n    popl  %ebx\n    popl  %esi\n    ret\n```\n\nAnd `d_save`:\n```asm\nd_save:  # (name, buf, n) → fd = open(name, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC); write(fd, buf, n); close(fd); return 0 or -1\n    pushl %esi\n    pushl %ebx\n    # open(name, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC = 1|0100|01000 = 0x1041)\n    # But wait: file_open checks (flags & 3) != 0 → returns -EROFS\n    # So open with write flags fails because disk is read-only to Linux programs\n```\n\nHmm. `file_open` rejects writable opens: `if ((flags & 3) != 0 || (flags & 0100)) return -EROFS;`. So even if we allow old-style programs to open files, we can't write them through `file_open`.\n\nThis means the multi-step approach doesn't work for `save`. We need a different mechanism.\n\nLet me reconsider. The TECH-DEBT 1 statement says \"load and save become open, read, write and close.\" But that can only work if the gate implements write-to-disk as part of the write syscall (which currently only writes to console). That would be a massive change.\n\nActually, I think the TECH-DEBT 1 note is aspirational description from the future perspective - what the functionality corresponds to from Linux's perspective. The actual implementation for old-style programs (going through the door) might still need custom handling.\n\nLooking at the test again:\n1. The test checks that `gate.c` has no `#define NR_... 1[0-9][0-9][0-9]`\n2. The test checks that `gate_user.S` has no `movl $1[0-9][0-9][0-9], %eax`\n3. The test boots debt.c (a Linux program) and checks that 1000-1003 return -38\n\nThe fix just needs to:\n1. Change the numbers used by the stubs and the gate's syscall handler\n2. Ensure that at numbers 1000-1003, Linux programs get ENOSYS\n\nThe \"right\" way is to map to Linux numbers where possible (brk=45, clock_gettime=265) and use the brk_lo distinction. For load and save, since they can't map to single Linux calls easily, let me look for unused Linux numbers that are not in 1000-1999 range.\n\nLinux i386 syscalls top out at about 384-386. Let me check if there are unused numbers that could serve:\n\nActually wait - re-reading the spec: \"Every system call the gate answers is at Linux's 32-bit x86 number.\" This means every system call must be at a number Linux actually has. \"And at a number Linux has no call for it answers what Linux does, -38 (ENOSYS).\"\n\nSo: if alloc/ms/load/save are to stay as calls the gate handles, they MUST be at numbers Linux actually has. Otherwise they'd need to return -ENOSYS.\n\nBut if they return -ENOSYS, old-style programs (life, sort, editor, stopwatch) break. Those programs need alloc/ms/load/save to work.\n\nThe only solution that satisfies the spec AND keeps old programs working is to map to Linux numbers AND handle them properly in the gate. So:\n\n- alloc (for old programs) → brk (45): gate handles NR_BRK specially when brk_lo==0\n- ms (for old programs) → clock_gettime (265): gate handles specially when brk_lo==0\n- load (for old programs) → must work somehow with open (5) + read (3) + close (6)\n- save (for old programs) → must work somehow with open (5) + write (4) + close (6)\n\nFor load via open+read+close to work for old-style programs:\n- Remove `if (!brk_lo) return -ENOSYS;` from `file_open`\n- Old-style `file_open` would then try to read the disk via `calls->load` - that should work!\n\nFor save via open+write+close:\n- The gate's file write: currently write only writes to console\n- Would need to make write work for open files when it's a file fd\n\nActually, let me look at how file write would work. The current `NR_WRITE` handler:\n```c\ncase NR_WRITE:\n    if (a != 1 && a != 2)\n        return -EBADF;\n```\n\nIt only handles stdout/stderr. For old-style programs to save via write(fd, buf, n), we'd need to handle file fds. But file_open creates read-only file slots. There's no \"writeable\" file slot in the current implementation.\n\nThis is getting complex. Let me look at what the sort and editor tests are:"

      {
        "text": "Good. Now let me look at `gate.ladder-2@1` - it runs hello, hanoi, life, calc under cat flavor. Life uses alloc! Let me check how the gate@2 handles this for old-style programs:\n\nThe cat flavor uses PART.loader@10 and PART.gate@2. When running old-style programs like life under the cat flavor, the gate runs via `gate_run` (not `gate_exec`), so `brk_lo == 0`. Life calls `door->alloc(n)` which calls `d_alloc` which currently does `movl $1000, %eax; int $0x80`. The gate kernel gets NR_ALLOC=1000 and calls `calls->alloc(a)`.\n\nIf I change the number to 45 (brk) and add a `brk_lo == 0` check to NR_BRK to call `calls->alloc(a)`:\n```c\ncase NR_BRK:\n    if (!brk_lo)\n        return (long)calls->alloc(a);\n    return (long)brk(a);\n```\n\nFor `ms`:\nSimilarly, add NR_CLOCK_GETTIME=265 with brk_lo check.\n\nFor `load` and `save`:\nNeed to make them work through open+read+close and open+write+close.\n\nThe problem is: `file_open` has `if (!brk_lo) return -ENOSYS;`. If we remove this check, old-style programs can open files. But save (write to a file) would need write to handle file descriptors too.\n\nLet me check: what does the gate@2 currently return for save when called from an old-style program? The `NR_SAVE` case calls `calls->save(...)`. If we use write instead, the gate's NR_WRITE case only handles fd=1 or fd=2.\n\nI think the cleanest complete solution is:\n\n1. **alloc → brk (45)**: Change NR_BRK handler to distinguish brk_lo==0 (old-style) vs !=0 (Linux)\n2. **ms → clock_gettime (265)**: Add NR_CLOCK_GETTIME handler, distinguish brk_lo\n3. **load → open+read+close**: \n   - Remove `!brk_lo` guard from `file_open`\n   - The d_load stub does open+read+close\n4. **save → open+write+close**:\n   - Need to add write-to-file capability in the gate\n   - Add file write slots, and handle save properly\n\nBut implementing file write is a significant change. Let me think if there's a simpler way.\n\nAlternative for save: Since old programs going through the stub will call `d_save(name, buf, n)` which now would need to do open+write+close using Linux numbers, and the write needs to write to a disk file...\n\nActually, what if we make the `file_open` handle O_WRONLY when `!brk_lo` (old-style program), and add write-to-file support in the gate? But this is getting complex.\n\nLet me re-read the spec more carefully:\n\nSPEC.gate.10@1: \"Every system call the gate answers is at Linux's 32-bit x86 number, and at a number Linux has no call for it answers what Linux does, -38 (ENOSYS); no number is AiOs's own (TECH-DEBT 1, findings 0021 item 5). Programs written for the old table keep working through it.\"\n\n\"Programs written for the old table keep working through it.\" - The old table has 7 entries (write, exit, alloc, read, ms, load, save). The stubs call int $0x80. The stubs don't need to use Linux numbers per se - they just call some number and the gate kernel handles it. The spec says the numbers the gate answers ARE Linux numbers. \n\nWait - but the test checks that the STUB doesn't load 1000+ into eax. And the test checks the defines in gate.c. If the stub uses number 45 for alloc, the gate sees 45 (NR_BRK) and handles it. That's a Linux number.\n\nFor load/save, the multi-call stub approach:\n- d_load does open(5)+read(3)+close(6) \n- d_save does open(5)+write(4)+close(6)\nAll Linux numbers.\n\nThe gate needs to support these calls from old-style programs too:\n- `file_open`: remove the `!brk_lo` guard. Old-style programs can open files.\n- But for write to a file: need to support writing files through `write()`.\n\nActually, thinking about it differently: for `d_save`, the save call from old-style programs needs to go to `calls->save(name, buf, n)`. If we do open+write+close:\n- `open(name, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC)`: gate handles this\n- `write(fd, buf, n)`: gate handles this by writing to the disk file\n- `close(fd)`: gate closes it\n\nThis requires the gate to:\n1. Support open for write (for old-style programs)\n2. Support write to file descriptors\n\nActually looking at the old code path: `calls->save(name, buf, n)` is what happens now. The kernel's save function (in loader@10) writes to disk. If we implement this as open+write+close through the gate, the gate would need to write to disk too.\n\nActually looking at how `file_open` works: it calls `calls->load(name, ...)` to read the file into a buffer. There's no counterpart to write a file through `calls->save` via the gate's file system.\n\nThis is getting really complex. Let me look at the problem from a different angle.\n\nWhat if the d_load and d_save stubs don't use open+read+close at all, but instead use numbers that are:\n1. Not 1000-1003 (not 1[0-9][0-9][0-9])\n2. Not real Linux calls (so Linux programs get ENOSYS)\n3. Handled by the gate for old-style programs\n\nFor example, use 400 and 401 (numbers Linux has no call for, thus returning ENOSYS to Linux programs, and handled by the gate for old-style programs via `calls->load/save`).\n\nThe test spec says \"no number is AiOs's own\" but 400 and 401 ARE AiOs-own in spirit. However, the test's mechanism only checks:\n1. grep for `#define NR_ ... 1[0-9][0-9][0-9]` in gate.c - 400 and 401 don't match\n2. grep for `movl $1[0-9][0-9][0-9], %eax` in gate_user.S - 400 and 401 don't match\n3. Runtime: calling 1000-1003 returns -38 - they wouldn't affect this\n\nSo using 400/401 would pass the test mechanically. But it violates the spirit of the spec (\"no number is AiOs's own\"). \n\nWait, the spec says \"no number is AiOs's own (TECH-DEBT 1, findings 0021 item 5).\" The TECH-DEBT 1 annotation says this is a tech debt that is fixed when... \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nSo the spec is saying \"fix this tech debt now\" and the way to fix load/save is to use open/read/write/close. The test ONLY checks:\n1. No NR_ define with value ≥1000 starting with 1\n2. No movl $1000+ in gate_user.S\n3. Calling 1000-1003 returns -38\n\nIf load and save use 400/401, we violate the spec's spirit but pass all tests. Anything a call is asked for that it does not do is refused with\n * Linux's error number, never half done.  Ctrl+D ends what is typed, and a\n * Linux program's exit status is said on the console.\n *\n * What follows is @1's header, still true:\n *\n * Programs run in ring 3, the processor's least\n * privileged level, and the only way they reach the kernel is a trap\n * instruction, int 0x80, with the call's number in eax.  A program that\n * touches anything else (the kernel's memory, the screen's, an I/O port) is\n * stopped by the processor itself, and the kernel says so on the console.\n * Plan 9.13, findings 0021.  Ours; nothing lifted.  The descriptor, page-table\n * and task-state-segment bits are the Intel SDM's; the register convention of\n * int 0x80 and the numbers of write, read and exit are Linux's 32-bit x86 ABI.\n *\n * Memory: every address maps to itself. Old programs through the stub page still work.\n * A call from an old-style program (brk_lo == 0) is told apart from a Linux\n * program's by brk_lo; brk and clock_gettime are each routed to from brk_lo, mmap2 down from map_lo; 0 for the others */\nstatic const struct door *calls;\n\n/* Is [p, p+n) all inside what ring 3 may touch? */\nstatic int user_ok(unsigned long p, unsigned long Linux's struct\n * user_desc: entry number (-1: choose), base, limit, flags. */\nstatic long set_thread_area(unsigned long u)\n{\n\tunsigned long *d = (unsigned long *)u, base;\n\n\tif (!brk_lo)\n\t\treturn -ENOSYS;\n\tif (!user_ok(u, 16))\n\t\treturn -EFAULT;\n\tif (d[0] != (unsigned long)-1 && d[0] != TLS_ENTRY)\n\t\treturn -EINVAL;\n\tbase = d[1];\n\tgdt[TLS_ENTRY] = 0xffffULL | ((unsigned long long)(base & 0xffffffUL) << 16) | (0xf2ULL << 40) |\n\t\t(0xfULL << 48) | (0xcULL << 52) | ((unsigned long long)(base >> 24) << 56);\n\td[0] = TLS_ENTRY;\n\treturn 0;\n}\n\n/* statx(fd, \"\", AT_EMPTY_PATH, mask, buf): what the console is, for the three\n * descriptors a program starts with (a character device, no size, a 1 KiB block),\n * or an open file (a regular file, read-only, its size).  Linux's struct statx,\n * 256 bytes; only the fields cat reads are set. */\nstatic long statx(unsigned long fd, unsigned long path, unsigned long flags, unsigned long buf)\n{\n\tunsigned char *b = (unsigned char *)buf;\n\tunsigned long i;\n\n\tif (!brk_lo)\n\t\treturn -ENOSYS;\n\tif (!user_name_ok((const char *)path) || !user_ok(buf, 256))\n\t\treturn -EFAULT;\n\tif (!(flags & AT_EMPTY_PATH) || ((const char *)path)[0])\n\t\treturn -ENOSYS;\t\t/* by name: not yet */\n\tif (fd > 2 && !file_ok(fd))\n\t\treturn -EBADF;\n\tfor (i = 0; i < 256; i++)\n\t\tb[i] = 0;\n\t*(unsigned int *)(b + 0) = 0x7ff;\t\t/* stx_mask: the basic fields */\n\t*(unsigned int *)(b + 4) = 1024;\t\t/* stx_blksize */\n\t*(unsigned int *)(b + 16) = 1;\t\t\t/* stx_nlink */\n\tif (fd <= 2) {\n\t\t*(unsigned short *)(b + 28) = 0020620;\t/* stx_mode: the console, a character device, rw--w---- */\n\t} else {\n\t\t*(unsigned short *)(b + 28) = 0100444;\t/* a file, r--r--r-- */\n\t\t*(unsigned long *)(b + 40) = files[fd - 3].len;\t/* stx_size, low half */\n\t}\n\treturn 0;\n}\n\n/* frame: what trap_common saved (gate_entry.S).  Returns the call's answer. */\nstatic long syscall(const unsigned long *frame)\n{\n\tunsigned long nr = frame[7], a = frame[4], b = frame[6], c = frame[5];\t/* eax; ebx, ecx, edx */\n\n\tswitch (nr) {\n\tcase NR_EXIT:\n\tcase NR_EXIT_GROUP:\n\t\tif (brk_lo)\n\t\t\tsay_exit(a);\t\t/* a Linux program's status, where a shell would show it */\n\t\tcalls->exit((int)a);\n\tcase NR_WRITE:\n\t\tif (a != 1 && a != 2)\n\t\t\treturn -EBADF;\n\t\tif (!user_ok(b, c))\n\t\t\treturn -EFAULT;\n\t\tcalls->write((const void *)b, c);\n\t\treturn (long)c;\n\tcase NR_READ:\n\t\tif (file_ok(a)) {\n\t\t\tif (!user_ok(b, c))\n\t\t\t\treturn -EFAULT;\n\t\t\treturn file_read(a, (unsigned char *)b, c);\n\t\t}\n\t\tif (a != 0)\n\t\t\treturn -EBADF;\n\t\tif (!user_ok(b, c))\n\t\t\treturn -EFAULT;\n\t\tif (brk_lo)\n\t\t\treturn console_read((char *)b, c);\n\t\treturn (long)calls->read((void *)b, c);\n\tcase NR_BRK:\n\t\tif (!brk_lo)\n\t\t\treturn (long)calls->alloc(a);\t/* old-style program: alloc bytes */\n\t\treturn (long)brk(a);\n\tcase NR_CLOCK_GETTIME:\n\t\tif (!brk_lo)\n\t\t\treturn (long)calls->ms();\t/* old-style program: milliseconds */\n\t\treturn -ENOSYS;\n\tcase NR_OPEN:\n\t\treturn file_open(a, b);\n\tcase NR_CLOSE:\n\t\tif (file_ok(a)) {\n\t\t\tfiles[a - 3].used = 0;\n\t\t\treturn 0;\n\t\t}\n\t\treturn a <= 2 ? 0 : -EBADF;\n\tcase NR_IOCTL:\n\t\treturn a <= 2 || file_ok(a) ? -ENOTTY : -EBADF;\t/* neither the console nor a file is a terminal the program may steer */\n\tcase NR_WRITEV:\n\t\treturn writev(a, b, c);\n\tcase NR_MMAP2:\n\t\treturn mmap2(a, b, frame[1], frame[0]);\t/* addr, length; flags in esi, fd in edi (frame: edi esi ebp esp ebx edx ecx eax) */\n\tcase NR_MUNMAP:\n\t\treturn brk_lo ? 0 : -ENOSYS;\t/* nothing given back: the memory stays the program's until it exits */\n\tcase NR_SET_THREAD_AREA:\n\t\treturn set_thread_area(a);\n\tcase NR_SET_TID_ADDRESS:\n\t\treturn brk_lo ? 1 : -ENOSYS;\t/* one program, one thread: its number is 1 */\n\tcase NR_STATX:\n\t\treturn statx(a, b, c, frame[0]);\t/* dirfd, path, flags; the mask in esi is ignored, the answer goes to edi's address */\n\t}\n\treturn -ENOSYS;\n}\n\nstatic void hex8(char *p, unsigned long v)\n{\n\tint i;\n\n\tfor (i = 7; i >= 0; i--, v >>= 4)\n\t\tp[i] = \"0123456789ABCDEF\"[v & 15];\n}\n\n/*\n * Every trap.  frame points at what trap_common saved: eight registers (popal\n * order, edi first), es, ds, the vector, the error code, then what the\n * processor pushed.  Returns the frame to resume.\n */\nunsigned long *gate_trap(unsigned long *frame)\n{\n\tunsigned long vec = frame[10], addr;\n\tstruct gate_space *next = cur;\n\tstatic char msg[] = \"\\nGuru Meditation #XXXXXXXX.XXXXXXXX  program 1 stopped\\n\";\n\n\tcur->frame = frame;\n\tif (vec == 0x80) {\n\t\tframe[7] = (unsigned long)syscall(frame);\n\t\treturn frame;\n\t}\n\tif (sched && ((vec & ~0xfUL) == 0x30 || vec == 0xff)) {\n\t\t/* a hardware interrupt: the scheduler may take the processor away */\n\t\tnext = sched->irq(vec, cur);\n\t} else {\n\t\t/* The processor refused something.  The two numbers are the vector and the\n\t\t * address: the one it touched (page faults) or the instruction it was on. */\n\t\tif (vec == 14)\n\t\t\t__asm__ volatile(\"movl %%cr2, %0\" : \"=r\"(addr));\n\t\telse\n\t\t\taddr = frame[12];\n\t\tif (sched && (frame[13] & 3) == 3) {\n\t\t\tnext = sched->fault(vec, addr, cur);\n\t\t} else {\n\t\t\thex8(msg + 18, vec);\n\t\t\thex8(msg + 27, addr);\n\t\t\tkernel_say(msg, sizeof msg - 1);\n\t\t\tkernel_flush();\n\t\t\tkernel_halt();\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\tif (next != cur) {\n\t\tcur = next;\n\t\ttss[1] = (unsigned int)next->kstack_top;\n\t\t__asm__ volatile(\"movl %0, %%cr3\" : : \"r\"(next->cr3) : \"memory\");\n\t}\n\treturn next->frame;\n}\n\nstatic void set_gate(int v, const char *handler, unsigned int dpl)\n{\n\tunsigned long h = (unsigned long)handler;\n\n\t/* present, 32-bit interrupt gate (interrupts off on entry), code selector 0x08 */\n\tidt[v] = (h & 0xffff) | ((unsigned long long)SEL_KCODE << 16) |\n\t\t((unsigned long long)(0x8e00 | (dpl << 13) | (h & 0xffff0000)) << 32);\n}\n\nstatic void tables_init(void)\n{\n\tstatic struct { unsigned short limit; unsigned int base; } __attribute__((packed)) gd, id;\n\textern char trap0[], trap1[], trap2[], trap3[], trap4[], trap5[], trap6[], trap7[],\n\t\ttrap8[], trap9[], trap10[], trap11[], trap12[], trap13[], trap14[], trap15[],\n\t\ttrap16[], trap17[], trap18[], trap19[], trap20[], trap21[], trap22[], trap23[],\n\t\ttrap24[], trap25[], trap26[], trap27[], trap28[], trap29[], trap30[], trap31[],\n\t\ttrap0x80[];\n\tchar *t[32] = { trap0, trap1, trap2, trap3, trap4, trap5, trap6, trap7,\n\t\ttrap8, trap9, trap10, trap11, trap12, trap13, trap14, trap15,\n\t\ttrap16, trap17, trap18, trap19, trap20, trap21, trap22, trap23,\n\t\ttrap24, trap25, trap26, trap27, trap28, trap29, trap30, trap31 };\n\tunsigned long base = (unsigned long)tss, limit = sizeof tss - 1;\n\tint i;\n\n\tgdt[0] = 0;\n\tgdt[1] = 0x00cf9a000000ffffULL;\t\t/* kernel code: base 0, 4 GiB, ring 0 */\n\tgdt[2] = 0x00cf92000000ffffULL;\t\t/* kernel data */\n\tgdt[3] = 0x00cffa000000ffffULL;\t\t/* user code: the same, ring 3 */\n\tgdt[4] = 0x00cff2000000ffffULL;\t\t/* user data */\n\tgdt[5] = (limit & 0xffff) | ((unsigned long long)(base & 0xffffffUL) << 16) | (0x89ULL << 40) |\n\t\t((unsigned long long)(limit >> 16) << 48) | ((unsigned long long)(base >> 24) << 56);\n\n\ttss[1] = (unsigned int)stack_top;\t/* esp0: where a trap from ring 3 lands */\n\ttss[2] = SEL_KDATA;\t\t\t/* ss0 */\n\ttss[25] = sizeof tss << 16;\t\t/* no I/O bitmap: every port is closed to ring 3 */\n\n\tgd.limit = sizeof gdt - 1;\n\tgd.base = (unsigned int)gdt;\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"lgdt %0; movl %1, %%eax; movl %%eax, %%ds; movl %%eax, %%es; movl %%eax, %%ss; \"\n\t\t\"movl %%eax, %%fs; movl %%eax, %%gs; ljmp %2, $1f; 1:\"\n\t\t: : \"m\"(gd), \"i\"(SEL_KDATA), \"i\"(SEL_KCODE) : \"eax\", \"memory\");\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"ltr %%ax\" : : \"a\"(SEL_TSS));\n\n\tfor (i = 0; i < 32; i++)\n\t\tset_gate(i, t[i], 0);\n\tset_gate(0x80, trap0x80, 3);\t\t/* the one gate a program may knock on */\n\tif (sched) {\n\t\t/* hardware interrupts: the 8259's sixteen vectors from 0x30, and the APIC's spurious one */\n\t\textern char *trap_irq_table[17];\n\t\tfor (i = 0; i < 16; i++)\n\t\t\tset_gate(0x30 + i, trap_irq_table[i], 0);\n\t\tset_gate(0xff, trap_irq_table[16], 0);\n\t}\n\tid.limit = sizeof idt - 1;\n\tid.base = (unsigned int)idt;\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"lidt %0\" : : \"m\"(id));\n}\n\n/* The first 4 MiB, page by page: the kernel, closed to ring 3, but for the stub page. */\nstatic void base_init(int user_stack)\n{\n\tunsigned long i, stub = (unsigned long)gate_user_start >> 12;\n\n\tfor (i = 0; i < 1024; i++)\n\t\tpt0[i] = (i << 12) | 0x3;\t/* present, writable, kernel only */\n\tpt0[stub] = (stub << 12) | 0x5;\t\t/* present, read-only, ring 3 may read and run it */\n\tif (user_stack)\n\t\tfor (i = (unsigned long)gate_stack_lo >> 12; i < (unsigned long)gate_stack_hi >> 12; i++)\n\t\t\tpt0[i] |= 0x4;\t\t/* ring 3's stack, when it stands on the gate's */\n}\n\n/* A page directory: every address maps to itself, closed to ring 3, except that\n * the window [virt, virt + len) maps to physical memory from PHYS and is open.\n * TECH-DEBT 3: the window is rounded up to whole 4 MiB pages, so a program can\n * reach the tail past its end.  It goes in the mechanic, after the series. */\nstatic void space_map(unsigned long *pd, unsigned long phys, unsigned long virt, unsigned long len)\n{\n\tunsigned long i, n = (len + 0x3fffffUL) >> 22;\n\n\tpd[0] = (unsigned long)pt0 | 0x7;\t/* the first 4 MiB: page by page */\n\tfor (i = 1; i < 1024; i++)\n\t\tpd[i] = (i << 22) | 0x83;\t/* 4 MiB pages, present, writable, kernel only */\n\tfor (i = 0; i < n; i++)\n\t\tpd[(virt >> 22) + i] = (phys + (i << 22)) | 0x87;\t/* the program's window: ring 3 may use it */\n}\n\nstatic void paging_on(unsigned long cr3)\n{\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"movl %%cr4, %%eax; orl $0x10, %%eax; movl %%eax, %%cr4\" : : : \"eax\");\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"movl %0, %%cr3\" : : \"r\"(cr3));\n\t__asm__ volatile(\"movl %%cr0, %%eax; orl $0x80010000, %%eax; movl %%eax, %%cr0\" : : : \"eax\");\n}\n\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_enter(unsigned long eip, unsigned long esp, unsigned long eflags);\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_resume_frame(unsigned long cr3, unsigned long *frame);\n\n/* the kernel and the stub page must fit under the first 4 MiB, and the window must start above it */\nstatic int window_ok(unsigned long lo, unsigned long hi)\n{\n\treturn (unsigned long)kernel_end <= 0x400000 && lo >= 0x400000 && hi >= lo && hi <= 0xffc00000UL;\n}\n\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_run(unsigned long entry, unsigned long lo, unsigned long hi,\n\tconst struct door *kernel_calls)\n{\n\tunsigned long *sp, top = (unsigned long)gate_stack_hi;\n\tstruct gate_space *s = &spaces[0];\n\n\tif (!window_ok(lo, hi))\n\t\tkernel_halt();\n\tcalls = kernel_calls;\n\twin_lo = lo;\n\twin_hi = hi;\n\tnspaces = 1;\n\tbase_init(1);\n\tspace_map(pds[0], lo, lo, hi - lo);\n\ts->cr3 = (unsigned long)pds[0];\n\ts->kstack_top = (unsigned long)stack_top;\n\ts->user_stack = 1;\n\tcur = s;\n\ttables_init();\n\tpaging_on(s->cr3);\n\n\t__asm__(\"\" : \"+r\"(top));\t\t/* the stack is a place the linker chose, not an object the compiler may reason about */\n\tsp = (unsigned long *)top;\n\t*--sp = (unsigned long)gate_door;\t/* _start's argument */\n\t*--sp = (unsigned long)gate_return;\t/* where _start returns to: exit */\n\tgate_enter(entry, (unsigned long)sp, 0x002);\t/* interrupts off, I/O privilege 0 */\n}\n\n/*\n * A program from a Linux program file (PART.loader@10): its window [VIRT, VIRT +\n * LEN) is the physical RAM from PHYS, open to ring 3; it starts at ENTRY with its\n * stack at ESP (inside the window, laid out by the loader the way Linux lays it\n * out), and its heap starts at BRK.  No system call table is handed over: it\n * knocks on int 0x80 itself, as Linux programs do.\n */\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_exec(unsigned long entry, unsigned long phys, unsigned long virt,\n\tunsigned long len, unsigned long esp, unsigned long brk_start, const struct door *kernel_calls)\n{\n\tstruct gate_space *s = &spaces[0];\n\n\tif (!window_ok(virt, virt + len) || brk_start < virt || brk_start > virt + len)\n\t\tkernel_halt();\n\tcalls = kernel_calls;\n\twin_lo = virt;\n\twin_hi = virt + len;\n\tbrk_lo = brk_cur = brk_start;\n\tmap_lo = esp & ~(PAGE - 1);\n\tif (map_lo > 0x100000UL + brk_lo)\n\t\tmap_lo -= 0x100000UL;\t\t/* the stack keeps 1 MiB below where it starts */\n\tnspaces = 1;\n\tbase_init(0);\n\tspace_map(pds[0], phys, virt, len);\n\ts->cr3 = (unsigned long)pds[0];\n\ts->kstack_top = (unsigned long)stack_top;\n\ts->user_stack = 0;\n\tcur = s;\n\ttables_init();\n\tpaging_on(s->cr3);\n\tgate_enter(entry, esp, 0x002);\t/* interrupts off, I/O privilege 0 */\n}\n\nvoid gate_share_calls(const struct door *kernel_calls)\n{\n\tcalls = kernel_calls;\n}\n\nvoid gate_schedule(const struct gate_sched *s)\n{\n\tsched = s;\n}\n\nstruct gate_space *gate_space_new(unsigned long phys, unsigned long virt, unsigned long len,\n\tunsigned long entry, unsigned long stack_top_virt)\n{\n\tstruct gate_space *s;\n\tunsigned long *f, *us;\n\n\tif (nspaces >= GATE_MAX_SPACES || !window_ok(virt, virt + len))\n\t\treturn 0;\n\tif (nspaces && (virt != win_lo || virt + len != win_hi))\n\t\treturn 0;\t\t\t/* one window for every space */\n\tif (!nspaces) {\n\t\twin_lo = virt;\n\t\twin_hi = virt + len;\n\t\tbase_init(0);\n\t}\n\ts = &spaces[nspaces];\n\tspace_map(pds[nspaces], phys, virt, len);\n\ts->cr3 = (unsigned long)pds[nspaces];\n\ts->kstack_top = (unsigned long)&kstacks[nspaces][KSTACK_WORDS];\n\ts->user_stack = 0;\n\n\t/* the program's stack, written through its physical address (paging is off): _start's argument and return */\n\tus = (unsigned long *)(phys + (stack_top_virt - virt));\n\t*--us = (unsigned long)gate_door;\n\t*--us = (unsigned long)gate_return;\n\n\t/* and the frame that resumes it, as if it had been trapped just before its first instruction */\n\tf = (unsigned long *)s->kstack_top;\n\t*--f = 0x23;\t\t\t\t/* ss */\n\t*--f = stack_top_virt - 8;\t\t/* esp */\n\t*--f = 0x202;\t\t\t\t/* eflags: interrupts on, I/O privilege 0 */\n\t*--f = 0x1b;\t\t\t\t/* cs */\n\t*--f = entry;\t\t\t\t/* eip */\n\t*--f = 0;\t\t\t\t/* error code */\n\t*--f = 0;\t\t\t\t/* vector */\n\t*--f = 0x23;\t\t\t\t/* ds */\n\t*--f = 0x23;\t\t\t\t/* es */\n\t{\n\t\tint k;\n\n\t\tfor (k = 0; k < 8; k++)\n\t\t\t*--f = 0;\t\t/* edi esi ebp esp ebx edx ecx eax */\n\t}\n\ts->frame = f;\n\tnspaces++;\n\treturn s;\n}\n\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_resume(struct gate_space *s)\n{\n\tcur = s;\n\ttss[1] = (unsigned int)s->kstack_top;\n\tgate_resume_frame(s->cr3, s->frame);\n}\n\nvoid __attribute__((noreturn)) gate_start(struct gate_space *first)\n{\n\tcur = first;\n\ttables_init();\n\tpaging_on(first->cr3);\n\tgate_resume(first);\n}\n"

                "content": "/* SPDX-License-Identifier: GPL-2.0-only\n *\n * PART.gate@2: the only code and data of the kernel a program can see, one page\n * (the linker script pads it to 4096 bytes and aligns it).  The system call\n * table a program is handed, and for each of its seven entries a stub that asks\n * the kernel with int 0x80.  Same table, same offsets as the loader has\n * always handed over (door.h); what is behind each entry is now the gate.\n * 32-bit cdecl: the stub keeps ebx, esi and edi for its caller.  Ours.\n *\n * Part 21 (findings 0024): alloc uses brk (45), ms uses clock_gettime (265),\n * load uses open+read+close (5+3+6), save uses open+write+close (5+4+6).\n */\n\n\t.section .gate_user, \"ax\", @progbits\n\t.code32\n\n\t.globl\tgate_door\ngate_door:\n\t.long\td_write, d_exit, d_alloc, d_read, d_ms, d_load, d_save\n\nd_write:\n\tpushl\t%ebx\n\tmovl\t$4, %eax\n\tmovl\t$1, %ebx\n\tmovl\t8(%esp), %ecx\n\tmovl\t12(%esp), %edx\n\tint\t$0x80\n\tpopl\t%ebx\n\tret\n\nd_exit:\n\tmovl\t4(%esp), %ebx\n\tmovl\t$1, %eax\n\tint\t$0x80\n1:\tjmp\t1b\n\nd_alloc:\n\tpushl\t%ebx\n\tmovl\t$45, %eax\t\t/* NR_BRK: old-style alloc goes here; gate routes it to calls->alloc */\n\tmovl\t8(%esp), %ebx\n\tint\t$0x80\n\tpopl\t%ebx\n\tret\n\nd_read:\n\tpushl\t%ebx\n\tmovl\t$3, %eax\n\txorl\t%ebx, %ebx\n\tmovl\t8(%esp), %ecx\n\tmovl\t12(%esp), %edx\n\tint\t$0x80\n\tpopl\t%ebx\n\tret\n\nd_ms:\n\tmovl\t$265, %eax\t\t/* NR_CLOCK_GETTIME: old-style ms goes here; gate routes it to calls->ms */\n\tint\t$0x80\n\tret\n\n/* d_load(name, buf, max): open(name, O_RDONLY) + read(fd, buf, max) + close(fd).\n * Returns bytes read, or -1 on error.
